<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Taking the limits in the required order

<details id="proof-stmt-vis-weight-lemma-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-vis-weight-lemma</summary>

<a id="vis:final-bound"></a>
*Proof of Proposition <a href="#vis:proposition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="vis:proposition">13</a>.* The union of the events $D_{st}$ contains $\{0\longleftrightarrow d\}$. Summing <a href="#vis:bridge" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="vis:bridge">[vis:bridge]</a> over the $O(w^2)$ pairs and applying <a href="#vis:row-connection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="vis:row-connection">[vis:row-connection]</a> gives $$c\,e^{-(d+a)e_N(w)}
 \le\sum_{s,t}\mathbb E_{\mathrm{tor}}X_{H_s}X_{H_t}.$$ Choose lifts with spatial center separation at most $w/2$. The joint test supports then have spatial extent at most $w/2+O(1)$ and time extent at most $d+O(1)$, both less than $3w/4$ for large $w$. The uniform moment comparison therefore replaces each torus correlation by its cutoff plane value with error $Ce^{-cw}$. For this *fixed* $w$, there are only finitely many pairs of centers, so plane moment convergence applies to all of them as $N\to\infty$. Their limiting correlations are bounded by $Ce^{-m_*d}$: every pair of test supports has time separation at least $d-O(1)$, and the kernel estimate <a href="#inp:decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inp:decay">[inp:decay]</a> applies to the fixed test shapes. Consequently $$\label{vis:final-bound}
 \limsup_{N\to\infty}e^{-(d+a)e_N(w)}
 \le Cw^2\bigl(e^{-m_*d}+e^{-cw}\bigr).$$

Write $L_w=\liminf_{N\to\infty}e_N(w)$. If $L_w<\infty$, take a subsequence on which $e_N(w)\to L_w$. Along it $ae_N(w)\to0$, so <a href="#vis:final-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="vis:final-bound">[vis:final-bound]</a> yields $$L_w\ge -\frac1d\log\!\left[
       Cw^2\bigl(e^{-m_*d}+e^{-cw}\bigr)\right].$$ If $L_w=\infty$, the desired lower bound already holds. Since $d=\lfloor\sqrt w\rfloor$, the displayed lower bound tends to $m_*$ as $w=w_k\to\infty$, proving <a href="#vis:gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="vis:gap">[vis:gap]</a>. ◻

</details>

In particular, given $v<m_*$, every sufficiently large fixed $w_k$ satisfies $e_N(w_k)>v$ for all sufficiently large $N$, with the cutoff threshold allowed to depend on $w_k$. This order of quantifiers is exactly what the finite doubling argument in Section <a href="#sec:counting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:counting">6</a> will use.

<a id="sec:counting"></a>
### A linear bound on the low-energy state count

Let $\mathcal N_{N,w}(u)$ count the transfer energies at circumference $w$ that are at most $u$, with multiplicity and including the vacuum. The visibility estimate from Section <a href="#sec:visibility" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:visibility">5</a> now allows us to turn the trace bounds into a count below the two-excitation threshold.

<a id="stmt-cou-linear"></a>
<div id="cou:linear" class="proposition">

**Proposition 16**. *There is a constant $C$ such that, for every sufficiently large dyadic circumference $w=w_k$, <a id="cou:linear-eq"></a>$$\label{cou:linear-eq}
 \limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathcal N_{N,w}(u_0)\le Cw,
 \qquad u_0=\frac32m_*.$$*

</div>

Set $u_1=13m_*/8$. The intermediate goal is the estimate $$\mathcal N_{N,2w}(y)
 \le 2(1+\varepsilon_w)\mathcal N_{N,w}(y+b_w),
 \qquad u_0\le y\le u_1,$$ with positive errors $b_w,\varepsilon_w$ summable over dyadic widths. For each sufficiently large fixed $w$, this estimate will hold for all sufficiently large $N$, with the cutoff threshold allowed to depend on $w$. Summability of the energy shifts keeps the iterated threshold inside the stated interval once the base width is large enough; the factors $1+\varepsilon_w$ have a finite product. Thus the factor $2$ at each doubling yields the desired linear growth in circumference.

To prove the estimate, we compare the heat trace at circumference $2w$ with the trace of two independent copies at circumference $w$. The energies in the latter system are sums $E+E'$. If such a sum lies below twice the gap, at least one component is the vacuum energy. A nonnegative polynomial approximation to an energy cutoff makes this observation quantitative without assuming convergence of individual eigenvalues.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Exchanging time and circumference

<a id="cou:exchange"></a>
<a id="cou:heat-product"></a>
Rotation of the partition function gives the exact identity $$\label{cou:exchange}
 e^{t g_N(w)}R_{N,w}(t)
   =e^{w g_N(t)}R_{N,t}(w),$$ where $g_N$ is the pressure correction from Section <a href="#sec:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:transfer">3</a>. For a large circumference $w$, set $$D=\lfloor w^{1/2}\rfloor,\qquad t_0=\lfloor D^{1/2}\rfloor.$$ Every integer physical time is an allowed lattice length. Uniformly in the cutoff and in the integers $t_0\le t\le D$, we claim that $$\label{cou:heat-product}
 \bigl|R_{N,2w}(t)-R_{N,w}(t)^2\bigr|\le Ce^{-cw}.$$ Indeed, applying <a href="#cou:exchange" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cou:exchange">[cou:exchange]</a> at $w$ and $2w$ yields $$\begin{aligned}
 \log R_{N,2w}(t)-2\log R_{N,w}(t)
  ={}&t\bigl(2g_N(w)-g_N(2w)\bigr)\\
    &+\log R_{N,t}(2w)-2\log R_{N,t}(w).
\end{aligned}$$ The first term is exponentially small in $w$ by <a href="#tra:trace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tra:trace">[tra:trace]</a>. The uniform positive gap at all sufficiently large circumferences, Corollary <a href="#tra:gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tra:gap">6</a>, gives a constant $c_*>0$ such that $$s_{N,t}(2w)\le s_{N,t}(w)
 \le e^{-c_*(w-t)}s_{N,t}(t)\le Ce^{-c'w}.$$ Here $s_{N,t}(t)$ is uniformly bounded by <a href="#tra:trace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tra:trace">[tra:trace]</a>. Thus the difference of logarithms is exponentially small. The same trace estimate bounds both $R_{N,2w}(t)$ and $R_{N,w}(t)^2$ uniformly on this time range: their logarithms are at most a polynomial in $w$ times $e^{-ct_0}$. Exponentiating proves <a href="#cou:heat-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cou:heat-product">[cou:heat-product]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### A positive approximation to an energy cutoff

For $u_0\le y\le u_1$, define $$b_w=D^{-1/8},\qquad
 j_0=\left\lceil D e^{-(y+b_w/2)}\right\rceil,
 \qquad
 Q_{w,y}(x)=\sum_{j=j_0}^{D}\binom Dj x^j(1-x)^{D-j}.$$ The polynomial is the upper tail of a binomial law, so $0\le Q_{w,y}(x)\le1$ on $[0,1]$. Its transition occurs between energies $y$ and $y+b_w$ when $x=e^{-E}$.

<a id="stmt-cou-polynomial"></a>
<div id="cou:polynomial" class="lemma">

<a id="cou:poly-low"></a>
<a id="cou:poly-high"></a>
**Lemma 17**. *For all sufficiently large $w$, uniformly in $y\in[u_0,u_1]$, $$\begin{aligned}
 Q_{w,y}(e^{-E})&\ge1-Ce^{-cD^{3/4}}
       &&(0\le E\le y),\label{cou:poly-low}\\
 Q_{w,y}(e^{-E})&\le Ce^{-cD^{3/4}}e^{-t_0E}
       &&(E\ge y+b_w).\label{cou:poly-high}
\end{aligned}$$ Every nonzero monomial of $Q_{w,y}$ has degree between $t_0$ and $D$, and the sum of the absolute values of its coefficients is at most $3^D$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cou-polynomial"><summary>Proof of stmt-cou-polynomial</summary>

*Proof.* The threshold $j_0/D$ differs from $e^{-y}$ and $e^{-(y+b_w)}$ by at least $c b_w$ on their respective sides. This follows from the mean value theorem, uniformly on the fixed energy interval, since the rounding error is at most $1/D=o(b_w)$. Hoeffding’s binomial tail bound (Hoeffding 1963, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[Hoeffding1963](#ref-Hoeffding1963)] therefore gives <a href="#cou:poly-low" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cou:poly-low">[cou:poly-low]</a> and <a href="#cou:poly-high" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cou:poly-high">[cou:poly-high]</a> without the factor $e^{-t_0E}$, because $Db_w^2=D^{3/4}$.

To retain a summable bound over all energies, that additional factor is essential. Choose $c_1>0$ such that $j_0\ge c_1D$ for every allowed $y$ and all large $w$. Fix $E_*>4\log(2)/c_1+1$. For $E\le E_*$, the loss $e^{t_0E_*}$ is absorbed by decreasing the constant in the exponent $-cD^{3/4}$, since $t_0=O(D^{1/2})$. For $E\ge E_*$, the binomial formula gives $$Q_{w,y}(e^{-E})\le 2^D e^{-j_0E}.$$ For large $w$, $t_0\le c_1D/2$, and consequently $$2^D e^{-(j_0-t_0)E}
 \le \exp\!\left(-\frac{c_1}{4}DE\right)
 \le Ce^{-cD^{3/4}}.$$ This proves <a href="#cou:poly-high" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cou:poly-high">[cou:poly-high]</a> for every $E\ge y+b_w$.

The smallest possible degree in the expanded polynomial is $j_0$, which is at least $t_0$ for large $w$. Finally its coefficient sum in absolute value is bounded by $$\sum_{j=j_0}^D\binom Dj 2^{D-j}\le3^D.$$ ◻

</details>

<a id="cou:poly-trace"></a>
Applying <a href="#cou:heat-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cou:heat-product">[cou:heat-product]</a> to each monomial gives $$\label{cou:poly-trace}
 \left|
   \sum_{E\text{ at }2w}Q_{w,y}(e^{-E})
   -\sum_{E,E'\text{ at }w}Q_{w,y}(e^{-(E+E')})
 \right|\le Ce^{-c'w}.$$ All sums count multiplicities. They converge absolutely, since the smallest degree is positive and the relevant heat traces are finite. The coefficient bound costs at most $3^D$, which is absorbed by $e^{-cw}$ because $D=O(w^{1/2})$.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
###### From polynomial traces to counts.

<a id="cou:recursion"></a>
By the visibility estimate <a href="#vis:gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="vis:gap">[vis:gap]</a>, for each sufficiently large fixed dyadic $w$ and all sufficiently large $N$ depending on $w$, $$2e_N(w)\ge\frac74m_*.$$ Increase the lower bound on $w$ so that $u_1+b_w<7m_*/4$. Any pair in <a href="#cou:poly-trace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cou:poly-trace">[cou:poly-trace]</a> with $E+E'<y+b_w$ then has a zero component. There are at most $2\mathcal N_{N,w}(y+b_w)$ such pairs. For the remaining pairs, Lemma <a href="#cou:polynomial" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cou:polynomial">17</a> bounds their total contribution by $$Ce^{-cD^{3/4}}\sum_{E,E'}e^{-t_0(E+E')}
 =Ce^{-cD^{3/4}}R_{N,w}(t_0)^2
 \le Ce^{-cD^{3/4}}.$$ Using <a href="#cou:poly-low" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cou:poly-low">[cou:poly-low]</a> for energies at $2w$ not exceeding $y$, and absorbing the additive errors into $\mathcal N_{N,w}\ge1$, proves $$\label{cou:recursion}
 \mathcal N_{N,2w}(y)
 \le2\bigl(1+Ce^{-cD^{3/4}}\bigr)
       \mathcal N_{N,w}(y+b_w),
 \qquad u_0\le y\le u_1.$$ The constants are independent of $N,w,y$; for each fixed $w$, the inequality holds for all sufficiently large $N$.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### Dyadic iteration

<details id="proof-stmt-cou-polynomial-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-cou-polynomial</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#cou:linear" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cou:linear">16</a>.* Choose a fixed dyadic base width $w_{k_0}$ large enough for the preceding estimates and so that $$\sum_{k=k_0}^{\infty}b_{w_k}<u_1-u_0.$$ This is possible because $b_{w_k}=O(w_k^{-1/16})$. The product of the factors $1+Ce^{-c\lfloor\sqrt{w_k}\rfloor^{3/4}}$ over the same range is finite. At the base width, $$\mathcal N_{N,w_{k_0}}(u_1)
 \le e^{w_{k_0}u_1}R_{N,w_{k_0}}(w_{k_0})\le C_0,$$ uniformly in the cutoff.

Fix a target $w_k$ with $k>k_0$. Apply <a href="#cou:recursion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cou:recursion">[cou:recursion]</a> backwards along the finite chain $w_k,w_{k-1},\ldots,w_{k_0}$, starting at threshold $u_0$. The accumulated threshold shifts remain below $u_1-u_0$, so every application is valid. For all sufficiently large $N$ on this finite chain, we obtain $$\mathcal N_{N,w_k}(u_0)
 \le C_0\,2^{k-k_0}
       \prod_{h=k_0}^{k-1}
         \left(1+Ce^{-c\lfloor\sqrt{w_h}\rfloor^{3/4}}\right)
 \le Cw_k.$$ Taking $\limsup_{N\to\infty}$ proves the assertion. The cutoff threshold may depend on the target width; no simultaneous limit in $N$ and $w$ has been used. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:mass-support"></a>
### From the state count to an isolated mass

Each mass in the support of $\rho$ contributes to the continuum two-point function throughout an interval of spatial momenta. We will test these contributions by positive measures from the finite-circumference transfer operator. Distinct masses will then require distinct low-energy states at every allowed momentum in that interval. Proposition <a href="#cou:linear" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cou:linear">16</a> bounds how many such masses there can be.

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### Measures at fixed momentum

Fix nonzero nonnegative smooth functions $g,h$ of compact support, with $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}g\subset(0,l)$ for a fixed positive integer $l$, and set $f(t,b)=g(t)h(b)$. We use color $3$ throughout. On a cylinder of circumference $w=w_k$, let $X_f$ be the source-normalized lattice field sum and define its bounded truncation by $$F=\max\{-w,\min\{X_f,w\}\}.$$ Use the slab $[0,l]$ for the normalized insertion $A_F$, with the initial row as its left kernel variable, and put $$v=A_F\Omega_{N,w}.$$ With this convention, the matrix element $\langle v,T_{N,w}^{n/a}v\rangle$ places the first copy of $F$ on $[-l,0]$ by time reflection and the second copy on $[n,n+l]$ by time translation. To see the corresponding time weights, let $Q_{h,N}$ denote row multiplication by $B_Na\sum_{b\in a\mathbb Z/w\mathbb Z}h(b)q_b^3$, using the periodization of $h$. Before truncation the linear insertion satisfies the exact formula $$A_{X_f}
   =a\sum_{t\in a\mathbb Z\cap[0,l]}
      g(t)T^{t/a}Q_{h,N}T^{(l-t)/a},
 \qquad
 A_{X_f}\Omega
   =a\sum_t g(t)T^{t/a}Q_{h,N}\Omega.$$

<a id="mass:finite-measure"></a>
Work in the complex transfer Hilbert space. Spatial row translations $U_b$, for $b\in a\mathbb Z/w\mathbb Z$, commute with $T_{N,w}$. Choose their character convention so that the momentum projection is $$\Pi_p=\frac{a}{w}\sum_{b\in a\mathbb Z/w\mathbb Z}e^{-ipb}U_b,
 \qquad
 p\in\frac{2\pi}{w}\mathbb Z\cap[-\pi/a,\pi/a).$$ For each such $p$, define the finite positive measure $$\label{mass:finite-measure}
 \nu_{N,w,p}
   =w\sum_E\bigl\|P_E\Pi_pv\bigr\|^2\delta_{e^{-E}}
 \quad\text{on }[0,1],$$ where $P_E$ is the transfer-energy projection. The factor $w$ converts the normalized character sum into a spatial Riemann sum.

Let $C_f(n,b)$ be the continuum correlation of $\phi^3(f)$ reflected in time with a second copy translated by $n$ in time and by $b$ in space. For a compact momentum interval $I$, we will write $\max_{p\in I}$ for the maximum over allowed lattice momenta lying in $I$.

<a id="stmt-mass-moments"></a>
<div id="mass:moments" class="lemma">

<a id="mass:moments-eq"></a>
**Lemma 18**. *For every integer $n\ge0$ and every compact interval $I$ with nonempty interior, $$\label{mass:moments-eq}
 \lim_{\substack{w\to\infty\\w=w_k}}\limsup_{N\to\infty}
 \max_{p\in I}
 \left|
   \int x^n\,\nu_{N,w,p}(dx)
   -\int_\mathbb Re^{-ipb}C_f(n,b)\,db
 \right|=0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-mass-moments"><summary>Proof of stmt-mass-moments</summary>

*Proof.* The spectral theorem and the character formula give $$\begin{aligned}
 \int x^n\,\nu_{N,w,p}(dx)
 &=w\langle v,T_{N,w}^{n/a}\Pi_pv\rangle\\
 &=a\sum_{\substack{b\in a\mathbb Z\\-w/2\le b<w/2}}
       e^{-ipb}\langle v,T_{N,w}^{n/a}U_bv\rangle.
\end{aligned}$$ The matrix element in the sum is the cylinder correlation of the two bounded slab observables just described. For fixed $f,n$ and large $w$, their joint support has time extent at most $n+2l$ and spatial extent at most $w/2+\operatorname{diam}(\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}h)$. Both are below $3w/4$. The cylinder-to-torus comparison, followed by Proposition <a href="#tra:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tra:comparison">8</a>, replaces this correlation by its cutoff plane counterpart with error at most $Cw^2e^{-cw}$, uniformly in $b$ and $N$. The uniform field tails then remove the two truncations on the plane with error $Ce^{-c'w}$, by Cauchy–Schwarz and the moment majorants. The factor $a$ times the number of spatial translates is $w$, so the total error in the displayed Riemann sum still tends to zero exponentially in $w$.

For a fixed $w$, the cutoff plane smeared correlations converge uniformly for $|b|\le w/2$ to $C_f(n,b)$. To see the uniformity, moment convergence gives pointwise convergence, while the difference-test bound <a href="#inp:test-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inp:test-difference">[inp:test-difference]</a> gives uniform equicontinuity. More explicitly, on a fixed compact translation interval the smooth translates satisfy $|f_b-f_{b'}|\le C|b-b'|$ on a fixed finite union of unit boxes. The second-moment majorant bounds the $L^2$ norm of the corresponding field difference by $C'|b-b'|$. Cauchy–Schwarz then gives the same modulus of continuity for the correlations. Compactness upgrades pointwise convergence to uniform convergence. Consequently, as $N\to\infty$, the Riemann sum of the cutoff plane correlations converges to the continuum integral over $[-w/2,w/2]$, uniformly for $p$ in $I$; the exponential factors have a uniform modulus of continuity on this compact set.

Finally, the separated-time kernel bound in Section <a href="#sec:inputs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:inputs">2</a> implies $|C_f(n,b)|\le Ce^{-c|b|}$ outside a fixed compact interval. The omitted spatial tail therefore tends to zero uniformly in $p$ as $w\to\infty$. This proves <a href="#mass:moments-eq" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mass:moments-eq">[mass:moments-eq]</a>. The case $n=0$ is included: $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}g$ has positive distance from zero, so the two reflected field supports remain separated in time. ◻

</details>

<a id="mass:measure"></a>
The Euclidean spectral representation <a href="#inp:euclidean" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inp:euclidean">[inp:euclidean]</a> identifies the limiting measures explicitly. Write $$\widehat h(p)=\int_\mathbb Re^{-ipb}h(b)\,db,
 \qquad G(E)=\int_0^l g(t)e^{-tE}\,dt,
 \qquad E(p,\mu)=\sqrt{p^2+\mu^2}.$$ For each $p\in\mathbb R$, let $\nu_p$ be the image, under $\mu\mapsto e^{-E(p,\mu)}$, of the positive measure $$\label{mass:measure}
 \frac{|\widehat h(p)|^2}{2E(p,\mu)}
       |G(E(p,\mu))|^2\,\rho(d\mu^2).$$ Integrating the kernel against the two slab tests and then Fourier transforming in their relative spatial displacement gives $$\int_\mathbb Re^{-ipb}C_f(n,b)\,db=\int x^n\,\nu_p(dx).$$ The time separation supplies exponential damping in $\mu$, so the integrals converge and depend continuously on $p$ on compact intervals. This also justifies the Fourier calculation, either directly or first as an identity of distributions and then of continuous functions.

<a id="mass:continuous-tests"></a>
We will need continuous spectral tests rather than moments. For every $\Psi\in C(I\times[0,1])$, Lemma <a href="#mass:moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mass:moments">18</a> implies $$\label{mass:continuous-tests}
 \lim_{\substack{w\to\infty\\w=w_k}}\limsup_{N\to\infty}
 \max_{p\in I}
 \left|
  \int\Psi(p,x)\,\nu_{N,w,p}(dx)
   -\int\Psi(p,x)\,\nu_p(dx)
 \right|=0.$$ Indeed, the $n=0$ case uniformly bounds the total masses in this iterated limit. Approximate $\Psi$ uniformly by polynomials in $x$ with continuous coefficients in $p$, for instance its Bernstein polynomials in $x$. Each coefficient is bounded on $I$, so Lemma <a href="#mass:moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mass:moments">18</a> applies to the finite sum; the uniform approximation controls the remainder against both measures. Thus no mass near $x=0$ is lost in passing from moments to continuous tests.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### Finiteness of the low mass support

<a id="stmt-mass-finite-support"></a>
<div id="mass:finite-support" class="proposition">

**Proposition 19**. *The set $$\{\mu\in[m_*,5m_*/4]:\mu^2\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\rho\}$$ is finite.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-mass-finite-support"><summary>Proof of stmt-mass-finite-support</summary>

*Proof.* Choose $p_0>0$ so small that $$\sqrt{p_0^2+(5m_*/4)^2}<u_0,
 \qquad \widehat h(p)\ne0\quad (|p|\le p_0).$$ This is possible because $5m_*/4<u_0=3m_*/2$ and $\widehat h(0)=\int h>0$. This momentum interval is fixed before any finite collection of masses is selected.

Choose $J$ distinct masses $\mu_1,\ldots,\mu_J$ from the indicated support. On $I=[-p_0,p_0]$, the curves $$x_j(p)=e^{-E(p,\mu_j)},\qquad j=1,\ldots,J,$$ are pairwise disjoint compact graphs in $I\times(e^{-u_0},1)$. Their mutual distances and their distances from the two horizontal boundary lines are positive. There are therefore nonnegative continuous functions $\Psi_j$ on $I\times[0,1]$, supported in mutually disjoint neighborhoods of these graphs contained in $I\times(e^{-u_0},1)$, and equal to one on the respective graphs.

For every $p\in I$, $$L_j(p):=\int\Psi_j(p,x)\,\nu_p(dx)>0.$$ Indeed, membership of $\mu_j^2$ in $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\rho$ gives positive measure to every neighborhood of that point. The integrand in <a href="#mass:measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mass:measure">[mass:measure]</a> is positive there: $\widehat h(p)\ne0$ and $G(E)>0$ for every $E>0$, since $g\ge0$ is nonzero. Continuity of $\Psi_j$ makes its value positive through a neighborhood of $\mu_j$ at the fixed momentum. Moreover $L_j$ is continuous in $p$, by exponential domination in <a href="#mass:measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mass:measure">[mass:measure]</a>. Compactness yields $$\eta:=\min_{1\le j\le J}\min_{p\in I}L_j(p)>0.$$ Both $\eta$ and the required approximation scale may depend on this finite collection of masses.

By <a href="#mass:continuous-tests" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mass:continuous-tests">[mass:continuous-tests]</a>, for all sufficiently large fixed dyadic $w$, and then all sufficiently large $N$ depending on $w$, $$\int\Psi_j(p,x)\,\nu_{N,w,p}(dx)>\eta/2
 \qquad (j=1,\ldots,J)$$ at every allowed $p\in I$. Each atom of the measure <a href="#mass:finite-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mass:finite-measure">[mass:finite-measure]</a> comes from a transfer-energy eigenspace with momentum $p$. The disjoint supports of the $\Psi_j$ therefore require at least $J$ distinct energies below $u_0$ in that space. Distinct momentum spaces are orthogonal. For large $w$, the number of momenta in $I$ is at least $c_0w$, where $c_0>0$ depends only on $p_0$; at any fixed $w$ these are distinct Brillouin characters once $N$ is large. Thus $$Jc_0w\le\mathcal N_{N,w}(u_0).$$ Taking the cutoff limit and using Proposition <a href="#cou:linear" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cou:linear">16</a> gives $Jc_0\le C$. The constants $c_0,C$ are independent of the selected masses and of $J$. The support can consequently contain only finitely many points in the stated interval. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-mass-finite-support-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-mass-finite-support</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#intro:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="intro:main">1</a>.* The nonempty closed mass support has minimum $m_*>0$, by the continuum inputs in Section <a href="#sec:inputs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:inputs">2</a>. Proposition <a href="#mass:finite-support" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mass:finite-support">19</a> makes this minimum isolated in the support. A positive locally finite measure assigns strictly positive finite mass to an isolated support point, so $$Z=\rho(\{m_*^2\})\in(0,\infty).$$ Choose $\delta>0$ small enough that no other mass in the support lies below $m_*+\delta$, and define $$m_1=m_*,\qquad \rho_{\mathrm{rest}}=\rho-Z\delta_{m_*^2}.$$ This is a positive measure, and its support is contained in $[(m_1+\delta)^2,\infty)$, as required. ◻

</details>

\\end{scope}\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Two schematic diagrams labeled (a) and (b). Panel (a) depicts a blue annular barrier formed by four overlapping strip crossings meeting at corner squares, with an overall width labeled 12 at the top and a vertical dimension labeled 2 for a corner square. Inside the central hollow region is an orange square labeled $s$, enclosed by a concentric blue boundary labeled $Q_1(s)$. Below panel (a) is the text \"(a) Four long-way crossings form a connected barrier.\" Panel (b) shows two annular barriers arranged horizontally under column headings \"row 0\" and \"row d\". The left barrier (at row 0) encloses an orange square test support labeled $s$, has an orange square labeled $h'_s$ on its right side, and is labeled below in blue as $x_C(H_s) \\ge \\sqrt{\\epsilon}$. The right barrier (at row d) encloses an orange square test support labeled $t$, has an orange square labeled $h'_t$ on its right side, and is labeled below in blue as $x_C(H_t) \\ge \\sqrt{\\epsilon}$. A red connecting path labeled $D_{st}$ spans between the two barriers. Below panel (b) is the text \"(b) The bridge meets both barriers, putting both weights in one cluster.\""
    }
